\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\,,

and this map clearly intertwines the left
{\mathfrak{F}}(I)
-action.
Taking inductive limits, we get an
{\mathfrak{F}}
-linear unitary
L^{2}({\mathfrak{F}},\phi)\cong\check{H}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}
.

We now construct a
natural unitary isomorphism
\mu_{z,c}:{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}\boxtimes_{\mathfrak{F}}\check{H}_{c}\to\check{H}_{z\rhd c}
,
where
{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}
is the DHR bimodule associated to
z\in Z({\mathcal{C}})
from §
6.2
above.
This isomorphism will clearly satisfy unital and associative coherences, which will endow
\check{H}
with the structure of a

Z({\mathcal{C}})
-module functor.
Observe that
Z({\mathcal{C}})\cong Z({\mathcal{C}})^{\rm rev}\cong Z({\mathcal{C}}^{\rm op})
as tensor categories where we have forgotten the braiding, and the forgetful functor
Z({\mathcal{C}}^{\rm op})\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm op}
is dominant.
Since
\check{H}_{1}\cong L^{2}({\mathfrak{F}},\phi)\in\mathsf{Rep}_{\phi}({\mathfrak{F}})
, this will show that the image of
\check{H}
lies in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\phi}
.

Now for every interval
I\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
containing the defect point for
{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}
, we have a unitary isomorphism

\mu_{z,c}(I):{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}(I)\boxtimes_{{\mathfrak{F}}(I)}\check{H}_{c}(I)={\mathcal{Y}}^{z}(I)\boxtimes_{\mathfrak{F}}H_{\overline{c}}(I)\to\check{H}_{z\rhd c}(I)=H_{\overline{c}\otimes F(\overline{z})}(I)

by gluing diagrams and bending the
z
-string down and to the right of
\overline{c}
:

The isomorphism
\mu_{z,c}(I)
clearly satisfies the unit and associativity axioms for a modulator.
Moreover, if
I\subset J
, we get a commutative square

\begin{matrix}{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}(J)\boxtimes_{{\mathfrak{F}}(J)}\check{H}_{c}(J)&\underset{\phantom{A}}{\xrightarrow{\mu_{z,c}(J)}}&\check{H}_{z\rhd c}(J)\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\hookrightarrow$}&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\hookrightarrow$}\\
{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}(I)\boxtimes_{{\mathfrak{F}}(I)}\check{H}_{c}(I)&\xrightarrow{\mu_{z,c}(I)}&\check{H}_{z\rhd c}(I).\end{matrix}

We thus get a well-defined unitary
\mu_{z,c}:=\varinjlim\mu_{z,c}(I):{\mathcal{Y}}^{z}\boxtimes_{\mathfrak{F}}\check{H}_{c}\to\check{H}_{z\rhd c}
, which endow
\check{H}
with the structure of a
Z({\mathcal{C}})
-module functor.

With these considerations, we can prove the following theorem.

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a UFC, let
{\mathfrak{F}}
be the associated fusion categorical net, and let
\phi=\phi_{1}
be the boundary state associatied to the trivial Q-system from Example
5.19
above.
The functor
\check{H}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathsf{Rep}_{\phi}({\mathfrak{F}})
from Construction
6.13
above is a
Z({\mathcal{C}})
-module equivalence onto
{\mathcal{M}}_{\phi}
.
In particular,
\phi
is a topological boundary.

Proof.

We first show
\check{H}
is fully faithful.
To show
\mathsf{Rep}({\mathfrak{F}})(\check{H}_{a}\to\check{H}_{b})\cong{\mathcal{C}}(\overline{b}\to\overline{a})\cong{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
for
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
we show that every
\mathsf{Rep}({\mathfrak{F}})
-intertwiner

T:\check{H}_{a}\to\check{H}_{b}

maps
\check{H}_{a}(I)\to\check{H}_{b}(I)
for every interval
I\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Since every
\check{H}_{-}(I)
is fully faithful, and since
\check{H}=\varinjlim\check{H}_{-}(I)
, this will prove the result.

For
I\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
and
n\geq\#I
, we define an orthogonal projection
q_{n}(I)
which is
\id_{X}
on sites in
I
and
\iota\iota^{\dagger}
on the
n
sites to the left and on the
n
sites to the right of
I
, where
\iota:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to X
is the inclusion.

q_{n}(I):=\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to77.22pt{\vbox to35.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.6113pt\lower-17.79164pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.32729pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{26.12547pt}{7.45863pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{26.12547pt}{-12.45863pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-30.78003pt}{7.45863pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-30.78003pt}{-12.45863pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}(n+I+n).

Since
T
intertwines the
{\mathfrak{F}}
-actions, we have that
Tq_{n}(I)=q_{n}(I)T
.
Thus if
x\in\check{H}_{a}(I)
,
x=q_{n}(I)x
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and thus

Tx=Tq_{n}(I)x=q_{n}(I)Tx
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We claim this means
Tx\in\check{H}_{b}(I)
.
Indeed,
\check{H}_{b}
is filtered by the finite dimensional subspaces
\check{H}_{b}(I)
, and thus we can write
\check{H}_{b}
as an orthogonal direct sum

\check{H}_{b}=\check{H}_{b}(I)\oplus\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}[\check{H}_{b}(n+I+n)\ominus\check{H}_{b}((n-1)+I+(n-1))].

Observe that the subspace
\check{H}_{b}(n+I+n)\ominus\check{H}_{b}((n-1)+I+(n-1))
on the right is given by adding a
\id_{X}-\iota\iota^{\dagger}
on both outer-most strands.
Now writing
Tx=(Tx)_{I}+\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}(Tx)_{n}
in this decomposition, we see that
Tx=q_{n}(I)Tx
for all
n
, and
q_{n}(I)(Tx)_{k}=0
for all
k=1,\dots,n-1
.
We conclude that
(Tx)_{n}=0
for all
n
, and thus
Tx=(Tx)_{I}\in\check{H}_{b}(I)
as claimed.

Now since
{\mathcal{M}}_{\phi}
was defined as the full
\rm W^{*}
-subcategory of
\mathsf{Rep}_{\phi}({\mathfrak{F}})
generated by
L^{2}({\mathfrak{F}},\phi_{1})
under the
\mathsf{DHR}({\mathfrak{F}})\cong Z({\mathcal{C}})
-action,
\check{H}
is dominant.
Since both
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\phi}
are unitarily Cauchy complete,
\check{H}
is a unitary equivalence.
∎

Appendix A
Path algebras and type
\rm III
factors

by Masaki Izumi

In this appendix authored by Masaki Izumi,
we further classify the type of the factor
{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime\prime}
for the boundary states
\psi_{\mathfrak{F}}
(see §
5.2
) of Levin-Wen models (defined in §
4
) in the type
\rm{III}
case.
We first prove a general result on the type of the von Neumann algebra
M=B^{\prime\prime}
generated by a certain KMS state
\phi
on an AF-algebra
B
defined in terms of a finite oriented graph
{\mathcal{G}}
.
The key point in the proof is that we can identify Krieger’s ratio set
[
Kri70b
]
, which gives us Connes’ invariant
S(M)

[
Con73
]
.
See
[
FM77b
, §2]
or
[
Tak03a
, §XIII.2]
for the equality between the two sets.
This result can then be applied to the setting of the Levin–Wen model, leading to a proof of the claim in Remark
5.14
.

A.1.
Path
\rm C^{*}
-algebras

Throughout this appendix, let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a finite oriented graph.
We write
{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}
for the set of vertices and edges respectively.
With
s,r:{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}\to{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
we denote the source and range maps.
We furthermore assume the choice of a distinguished vertex
\ast\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
.
For simplicity, we make the following assumption:

•

For any pair of vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
, there is an edge
e\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}
with
s(e)=v_{1}
and
r(e)=v_{2}
.

We define

\Path^{n}_{v}({\mathcal{G}}):=\left\{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\cdots\xi_{n}\middle|r(\xi_{i})=s(\xi_{i+1}),\,\,s(\xi_{1})=v\right\}

as the set of all paths of length
n
starting at the vertex
v
.
For any finite path
\xi
, we will write
|\xi|
for its length, and
r(\xi)
for the range of the last edge in the path.

The paths starting in the distinguished vertex
*
can be used to define a tower of finite dimensional
\rm C^{*}
-algebras
(B_{n})
as in
[
Ocn88
,
GdlHJ89
,
EK98
,
JS97
,
JP11
]
.
For each pair
\xi,\eta\in\Path^{n}_{\ast}({\mathcal{G}})
, define the formal symbol
\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
and let

B_{n}:=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|\middle|\xi,\eta\in\Path_{\ast}^{n}({\mathcal{G}}),\,\,r(\xi)=r(\eta)\right\},

with multiplication and
*
-structure given by

\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi_{2}\right|\cdot\left|\eta_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|:=\delta_{\xi_{2}=\eta_{1}}\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|\qquad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\qquad\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|^{*}:=\left|\eta\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|.

Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is finite,
B_{n}
is a
\rm C^{*}
-algebra
which is the direct sum of
\left|{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}\right|
full matrix algebras.
For each
v\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
, the corresponding summand is an
n_{v}\times n_{v}
matrix algebra, where
n_{v}
is the number of paths from
\ast
to
v
of length
n
.
One can think of
\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
as a loop of length
2n
on
{\mathcal{G}}
starting at
*
where you first travel
\xi
to
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
, and then travel
\eta
in the reverse direction.
This gives the loop algebra convention of
[
JP11
]
.

The inclusion
\iota_{n}:B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n+1}
is given by

(A.1)

\iota_{n}\left(\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi_{2}\right|\right):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(\xi_{1})=s(\eta)\\
|\eta|=1\end{subarray}}\left|\xi_{1}\eta\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi_{2}\eta\right|.

That is, we extend each pair of paths by one edge in all possible ways satisfying the loop condition, and sum the result.
By our assumptions on
{\mathcal{G}}
,
\iota_{n}:B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n+1}
is injective and unital, and the Bratteli diagram can be identified with
{\mathcal{G}}
.
We write

B:=\varinjlim_{n\to\infty}B_{n}

for the corresponding AF-algebra.

A.2.
Edge and vertex weighting and a KMS state

To define a KMS state on
B
we need to specify a weight on our graph
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Definition A.2
.

Consider a
weight

w:{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}\cup{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}\to(0,\infty)
on the vertices and edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
.
Then for
\xi\in\Path^{n}_{v}({\mathcal{G}})
, we set
w(\xi):=w(\xi_{1})w(\xi_{2})\cdots w(\xi_{n})
.

We now assume that our weight
w
on
{\mathcal{G}}
satisfies the following condition:

•

there is a
\delta>0
satisfying that for all
v\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
, we have

(A.3)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\eta|=1\\
s(\eta)=v\end{subarray}}w(\eta)w(r(\eta))=\delta w(v).

In the final subsection of this appendix we will give an example of such a weight.

Remark A.4
.

Observe the condition above is
a system of linear equations, with one equation and variable for each vertex
v\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
.
So any solution of this system is a formula for the weights of the vertices as a function of the weights of the edges, with possibly some free parameters.
If all edges have weight 1, then
w
is a Frobenius-Perron eigenvector associated to
\delta
.
If all vertices are weighted 1, this is roughly the
\delta
-fairness condition of
[
DCY15
]
(without the balancing and even number of loops conditions).

Let us now introduce dynamics on
B
.
For
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|\in B_{n}
, define

(A.5)

\sigma_{t}(\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|):=\frac{w(\xi)^{it}}{w(\eta)^{it}}\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|.

This induces a one-parameter group of
*
-automorphism on
B_{n}
which is compatible with the inclusion
B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n+1}
defined above.
Hence we can extend it to a one-parameter group
\sigma_{t}\in\Aut(B)
.

Next define a state
\phi
on
B_{n}
by

(A.6)

\phi(\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|):=\delta_{\xi=\eta}\frac{1}{\delta^{n}}w(\xi)w(r(\xi)),\quad\quad\xi,\eta\in\Path^{n}_{\ast}({\mathcal{G}}).

Using
A.3
,
it is straightforward to see that

\phi
is a positive linear functional on
B_{n}
with
\phi(1)=1
which is compatible with the inclusion
B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n+1}
.
Hence
\phi
extends uniquely to a state on
B
by Corollary
2.19
.

Furthermore, observe that every
\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
is entire with respect to
\sigma_{t}
, and when
\beta=-1
,

\displaystyle\phi(\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{1}\right|\cdot\sigma_{-i}(\left|\xi_{2}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|))

\displaystyle=\frac{w(\xi_{2})}{w(\eta_{2})}\phi(\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{1}\right|\cdot\left|\xi_{2}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\eta_{1}=\xi_{2}}\delta_{\xi_{1}=\eta_{2}}\frac{w(\xi_{2})}{\delta^{n}w(\eta_{2})}w(\xi_{1})w(r(\xi_{1}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{\eta_{1}=\xi_{2}}\delta_{\xi_{1}=\eta_{2}}\frac{1}{\delta^{n}}w(\xi_{2})w(r(\eta_{2}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{\eta_{1}=\xi_{2}}\delta_{\xi_{1}=\eta_{2}}\frac{1}{\delta^{n}}w(\xi_{2})w(r(\xi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\phi(\left|\xi_{2}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|\cdot\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{1}\right|)

for all
\left|\xi_{1}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{1}\right|,\left|\xi_{2}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|\in B_{n}

(recall here that
r(\xi_{2})=r(\eta_{2})
as
\left|\xi_{2}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta_{2}\right|\in B_{n}
).

Since the Bratteli diagram for
B
is connected and stationary, by
[
Kis00
, Prop. 4.1]
and uniqueness of the Frobenius-Perron eigenvector (up to scaling),
\phi
is the unique
\beta=-1
KMS state for the dynamics
\sigma
.

We have just proved the following proposition.

Proposition A.7
.

The state
\phi
is the unique
\beta=-1
KMS state on
B
for the dynamics
\sigma
.
Thus
M:=B^{\prime\prime}
acting on
L^{2}(B,\phi)
is a factor.

Note that
B
is simple because
{\mathcal{G}}
is connected.
Hence as before (see §
5.3
),
\phi
extends to a faithful normal state on
B^{\prime\prime}
acting on
L^{2}(B,\phi)
, and the extension of
\sigma
to
B^{\prime\prime}
corresponds to the modular automorphism group with respect to the canonical cyclic vector in
L^{2}(B,\phi)
.

A.3.
Type of the von Neumann completion using Krieger’s ratio set

Recall that a maximal abelian von Neumann subalgebra
A
of a von Neumann algebra
M
is called a
Cartan subalgebra
if
there exists a faithful normal conditional expectation
E:M\to A

and its normalizer generates
M
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(A)^{\prime\prime}=M
where

{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(A):=\left\{u\in{\mathcal{U}}(M)\middle|uAu^{*}=A\right\}.

This latter condition, called
regularity
of
A\subset M
, is equivalent to

M=A\vee{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(A)
.

We now consider the von Neumann algebra
M:=B^{\prime\prime}
in the GNS representation
L^{2}(B,\phi)

and the abelian von Neumann subalgebra

A:=\left\{\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|\middle|\xi\in\bigcup_{n}\Path_{\ast}^{n}(\mathcal{G})\right\}^{\prime\prime}.

Lemma A.8
.

A
is a Cartan subalgebra of
M
.

Proof.

Clearly
A
is abelian.
Note that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the algebra
B_{n}
is globally fixed by all
\sigma_{t}
, that is
\sigma_{t}(B_{n})=B_{n}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Since
\sigma
is the modular automorphism group for
\phi
, by a theorem of Takesaki
[
Tak03b
, Thm. IX.4.2]
this implies the existence of a sequence of normal conditional expectations
E_{n}:M\to B_{n}
such that
\phi\circ E_{n}=\phi
.
Then for all
x\in M
, we have that
E_{n}(x)\to x
in the strong-
*
topology.
Indeed, to see this, recall that on the unit ball of
M
, the topology induced by
\|\cdot\|_{\phi}
coincides with the
\sigma
-strong topology
[
Tak02
, Prop. III.5.4]
.
It follows that for
x\in M
, we can choose
(x_{i})\in\bigcup_{n}B_{n}
with
\|x_{i}-x\|_{\phi}\to 0
.
Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n}\|E_{n}(x)-x\|_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n}\left\|E_{n}(x-x_{i})-(x-x_{i})+E_{n}(x_{i})-x_{i}\right\|_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|x-x_{i}\|_{\phi}+\underbrace{\lim_{n\to\infty}\|E_{n}(x_{i})-x_{i}\|_{\phi}}_{=0}

for all
i
, and similarly for
x^{*}
.
Hence
\|E_{n}(x)-x\|_{\phi}+\|E_{n}(x^{*})-x^{*}\|_{\phi}\to 0
for all
x\in M
.
Since
(E_{n}(x)-x)_{n}
and
(E_{n}(x^{*})-x^{*})_{n}
are both norm-bounded sequences, the claim follows.

Now consider
a^{\prime}\in M\cap A^{\prime}
, so that
E_{n}(a^{\prime})\in(A\cap B_{n})^{\prime}\cap B_{n}
.
Because
A\cap B_{n}
is maximal abelian in
B_{n}
, it follows that
E_{n}(a^{\prime})\in A\cap B_{n}
, and from the strong-
*
convergence of
E_{n}(a^{\prime})
to
a^{\prime}
, it follows that
a^{\prime}\in A
, and hence
A
is a MASA.

Since
A
is also globally fixed by all
\sigma_{t}
, as above there exists a normal conditional expectation
E:M\to A
such that
\phi\circ E=\phi
.
Since
\phi
is faithful, it follows that
E
is faithful as well.

It remains to check that
A\subset M
is regular.
For each pair of paths
\xi,\eta\in\Path_{*}^{n}({\mathcal{G}})
with
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
but
\xi\neq\eta
, define the operator

(A.9)

u_{\xi,\eta}:=\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|+\left|\eta\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|+1-\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|-\left|\eta\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|.

It is straightforward to check that these are self-adjoint unitaries, and that
u_{\xi,\eta}Au_{\xi,\eta}^{*}=A
, and therefore
u_{\xi,\eta}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(A)
.
Finally, note that
u_{\xi,\eta}\left|\eta\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|=\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
, from which it is clear that
A
together with the
u_{\xi,\eta}
generate
M=B^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

By the Feldman–Moore Theorem
[
FM77b
, Thm. 1]

M\cong L{\mathcal{R}}
where
{\mathcal{R}}
is a countable standard relation on a Borel measure space
\Omega

and

A\cong L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
.
We will show this explicitly for
M
by identifying a measure space
(\Omega,\mu)
such that
A=L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
, together with an action
G\curvearrowright\Omega
, where
G
is the group generated by the unitaries of (
A.9
) in Lemma
A.8
.
The equivalence relation
{\mathcal{R}}
is then the orbit equivalence relation.
This explicit description makes it possible to compute Krieger’s ratio sets and determine the type of
M
.

We first claim that
A=L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
, where we define the measure space
\Omega
as follows.
Let
\Omega
be the Cantor set
\Path_{*}^{\infty}({\mathcal{G}})
of infinite paths on
{\mathcal{G}}
.
For
\xi\in\Path_{*}^{n}({\mathcal{G}})
, define the cylinder set

E_{\xi}:=\left\{\eta\in\Omega\,\,\middle|\,\,\xi_{i}=\eta_{i}\text{ for all }i\leq|\xi|\right\}.

For two finite paths
\xi,\eta
starting at
*
and
|\xi|\leq|\eta|
we have
E_{\xi}\cap E_{\eta}=\emptyset
if the first
|\xi|
edges of
\eta
do not agree with those of
\xi
, or
E_{\eta}\subset E_{\xi}
otherwise.
Thus the cylinder sets form a basis for a topology on
\Omega
.

Let
\chi_{E_{\xi}}
be the indicator function for a cylinder set.
Then we can map
\chi_{E_{\xi}}\mapsto\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|\in A
, and this is compatible with multiplication on both sides:

\chi_{E_{\xi}}\cdot\chi_{E_{\eta}}=\chi_{E_{\xi}\cap E_{\eta}}\mapsto\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|\cdot\left|\eta\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
.
For
\xi\in\Path^{n}_{*}({\mathcal{G}})
, define

(A.10)

\mu(E_{\xi}):=\phi(\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|)=\frac{1}{\delta^{n}}w(\xi)w(r(\xi)).

Since
\phi
is a normal state, it follows from the discussion above that
\mu
extends to a regular Borel measure on
\Omega
.
The map
\pi:=\chi_{E_{\xi}}\mapsto\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\xi\right|
mentioned earlier extends to a
*
-homomorphism of a (SOT) dense subalgebra of
L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
onto a dense subalgebra of
A
.
Write
\mu
again for the state on
L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
induced by the probability measure.
Since
\mu(f)=\phi(\pi(f))
on this dense subalgebra and both states are normal and faithful, it follows that
\pi
extends to a spatial isomorphism of von Neumann algebras, and we may write
A=L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
.

Now let
G
be the (countable) group generated by our normalizing unitaries
u_{\xi,\eta}
as in (
A.9
)
which together with
A
generate
M
.
We now identify the action of
G
on
A
under the identification
A=L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mu)
.
Since
M=L{\mathcal{R}}
acts on
L^{2}(\Omega,\mu)
,
we first identify the action of the ket-bra operators

\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
for
\xi,\eta\in\Path_{*}^{n}(\mathcal{G})
such that
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
.
Given
\omega\in\Omega
, we can write
\omega=\zeta\zeta^{\prime}
, where
\zeta
consists of the first
n
edges in the path, so
\omega\in E_{\zeta}
, and
\zeta^{\prime}
the rest of the infinite path.
We have

\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|\cdot\omega=\begin{cases}\xi\zeta^{\prime}&\text{if }\eta=\zeta,\text{ equivalently }\omega\in E_{\eta}=E_{\zeta}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and thus
the action of (the generators of)
G
on
\Omega
is given as follows:

(A.11)

u_{\xi,\eta}\cdot\omega:=\begin{cases}\eta\zeta^{\prime}&\textrm{if }\zeta=\xi,\text{ equivalently }\omega\in E_{\xi}=E_{\zeta}\\
\xi\zeta^{\prime}&\textrm{if }\zeta=\eta,\text{ equivalently }\omega\in E_{\eta}=E_{\zeta}\\
\omega&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular,
u_{\xi,\eta}
sends single points in
\Omega
to single points, since
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
.
Note that
u_{\xi,\eta}
replaces the first part of the path
\omega
with the path
\eta
(resp.
\xi
) if
\omega
starts with the path
\xi
(resp.
\eta)
.
In all other cases it acts trivially.
Thus (
A.11
) is indeed a
G
-action on
\Omega
.
Since the cylinder sets generate the topology on
\Omega
,
G
acts by Borel automorphisms on
\Omega
,
and we have the standard Borel orbit equivalence relation

{\mathcal{R}}:=\left\{(\omega,g\cdot\omega)\,\middle|\,g\in G,\,\omega\in\Omega\right\},

which is a Borel subset of
\Omega\times\Omega
.
Note that two elements in
\Omega
are equivalent if and only if the corresponding infinite paths differ in finitely many places only.

For every
u_{\xi,\eta}\in G
, we define a new measure via
u_{\xi,\eta}\cdot\mu(E):=\mu(u_{\xi,\eta}^{-1}\cdot E)
as usual.
The Radon–Nikodym derivatives are given by

(A.12)

\frac{d(u_{\xi,\eta}\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}(\omega)=\begin{cases}\frac{w(\eta)}{w(\xi)}&\omega\in E_{\xi}\\
\frac{w(\xi)}{w(\eta)}&\omega\in E_{\eta}\\
1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

which can be directly verified using (
A.10
) and (
A.11
).
In particular, it follows that
\mu
is quasi-invariant with respect to the action of
G
.

To compute Connes’ invariant
S(M)
it suffices to determine Krieger’s ratio set
r(\Omega,G,\mu)

[
Kri70a
]
.
We recall the definition here (adapted slightly to the case at hand).

Definition A.13

(
[
Kri70a
]
)
.

The
ratio set

r(\Omega,G,\mu)
is the set of all
r\in[0,\infty)
such that for all measurable
A\subset\Omega
with
\mu(A)>0
and
\epsilon>0
, there is a measurable
B\subset A
with
\mu(B)>0
together with a
g\in G
such that
g\cdot B\subset A
satisfying that

(A.14)

\left|\frac{d(g\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}(\omega)-r\right|<\epsilon

for almost every
\omega\in B
.

We claim that the ratio set is generated by quotients of the form
w(\xi)/w(\eta)
, where
\xi
and
\eta
are finite paths starting in
*
and ending in the same vertex.
To prove this we will use the following lemma.

Lemma A.15
.

For all
\xi,\eta\in\Path_{*}^{n}({\mathcal{G}})
with
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
, there is an
\ell>0
such that for all cylinder sets
E_{\zeta}
, the following holds:
There are
A,B\subset E_{\zeta}
, together with a
g\in G
with
g^{2}=1
such that
g\cdot A=B
and the following bounds are satisfied:

\mu(A)\geq\ell\mu(E_{\zeta})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(B)\geq\ell\mu(E_{\zeta}).

Moreover,
A,B
and
g
can be chosen such that we have

\frac{d(g\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}(\omega)=\begin{cases}\frac{w(\xi)}{w(\eta)}&\text{for }\omega\in A\\
\frac{w(\eta)}{w(\xi)}&\text{for }\omega\in B,\end{cases}

and
g
acts trivially on
E_{\zeta^{\prime}}
if
E_{\zeta}\cap E_{\zeta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

For each vertex
v\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
, choose an edge
e_{v,*}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}
going from
v
to
*
.
Define

\ell:=\frac{1}{\delta^{n+1}}\frac{m}{M}w(r(\eta))\cdot\min\{w(\xi),w(\eta)\},

where
m:=\min_{v\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}}w(e_{v,*})
is the minimum weight of the edges ending in the distinguished point and
M:=\max_{v\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}}w(v)
the maximum vertex weight.
Define the two sets
A:=E_{\zeta e_{r(\zeta),*}\eta}
and
B:=E_{\zeta e_{r(\zeta),*}\xi}
.
Then clearly
A,B\subset E_{\zeta}
.
Using (
A.10
) it follows that

\frac{\mu(A)}{\mu(E_{\zeta})}=\frac{1}{\delta^{n+1}}\frac{w(e_{r(\zeta),*})w(\eta)w(r(\eta))}{w(r(\zeta))}\geq\ell.

Since
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
, we obtain
\mu(B)\geq\ell\mu(E_{\zeta})
similarly.

Now set
g:=u_{\zeta e_{r(\zeta),*}\xi,\zeta e_{r(\zeta),*}\eta}
.
Then
g\cdot E_{\zeta}\subset E_{\zeta}
,
g\cdot A=B
, and
g^{2}=1
.
The claim on the Radon–Nikodym derivative follows from (
A.12
).
Finally, if
E_{\zeta}\cap E_{\zeta^{\prime}}=\emptyset
,
our choice of
g
acts trivially on
E_{\zeta^{\prime}}

by (
A.11
).
∎

Theorem A.16
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\xi,\eta\in\Path_{v}^{n}({\mathcal{G}})
with
r(\xi)=r(\eta)
, we have
\frac{w(\xi)}{w(\eta)}\in r(\Omega,G,\mu)
.

Proof.

We first consider the case where
v=*
.
Let
E\subset\Omega
be a Borel set with
\mu(E)>0
.
Since
\mu
is regular, for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
U\subset\Omega
open such that
E\subset U
and
\mu(U\setminus E)<\epsilon\mu(E)
.
The cylinder sets generate the topology of
\Omega
, and since two cylinder sets are either disjoint or one is contained in the other, it follows that there is a sequence
(\xi_{k})\subset\bigcup_{n}\Path_{*}^{n}({\mathcal{G}})
such that

U=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}E_{\xi_{k}},\qquad\qquad E_{\xi_{i}}\cap E_{\xi_{j}}=\emptyset\text{ whenever }i\neq j,

where we allow
E_{\xi_{k}}
to be the empty set.
Choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mu(U)-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu(E_{\xi_{k}})<\epsilon\mu(E)
and set
E^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}E_{\xi_{k}}
.
Then

\mu(E\Delta E^{\prime})=\mu(E\setminus E^{\prime})+\mu(E^{\prime}\setminus E)\leq\mu(U\setminus E^{\prime})+\mu(U\setminus E)<2\epsilon\mu(E).

For each
k=1,\dots,m
, choose sets
A_{k},B_{k}\subset E_{\xi_{k}}
and a group element
g_{k}
as in the statement of Lemma
A.15
.
Set
A:=\bigcup_{k}A_{k}
and
B:=\bigcup_{k}B_{k}
.
Since the sets
E_{\xi_{k}}
are disjoint it follows that

\mu(A)\geq\ell\mu(E^{\prime})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(B)\geq\ell\mu(E^{\prime}),

where
\ell
is the constant from Lemma
A.15
(note that it only depends on the choice of
\xi,\eta
).
Let
g=g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{m}

(since the
g_{k}
act non-trivially only on mutually disjoint sets,
they all commute).
Again by Lemma
A.15
,

\frac{d(g\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}(\omega)=\begin{cases}r&\text{for }\omega\in A\\
r^{-1}&\text{for }\omega\in B,\end{cases}

where we set
r:=w(\xi)/w(\eta)
.

Using the results so far, we obtain the following estimates:

\displaystyle\mu((E\cap A)\Delta A)\leq\mu(E\setminus E^{\prime})<2\epsilon\mu(E)

\displaystyle\mu(g(E\cap B)\Delta A)=\mu(g((E\cap B)\Delta B))=\frac{1}{r}\mu((E\cap B)\Delta B)<\frac{2\epsilon}{r}\mu(E)

\displaystyle\mu(A)\geq\ell\mu(E^{\prime})>\ell(\mu(U)-\epsilon\mu(E))\geq\ell(1-\epsilon)\mu(E).

In the second line, we used that
gA=B
and
g^{2}=1
in the first equality, and that the Radon–Nikodym derivative is equal to
r^{-1}
on
B
in the second equality.
The last inequality in the last line follows because
E\subset U
.

Define the following sets:

A_{1}:=E\cap A\qquad\text{ and }\qquad A_{2}:=g(E\cap B)\cap A.

Then clearly
\mu(A)\geq\mu(A_{1})+\mu(A_{2})-\mu(A_{1}\cap A_{2})
.
Without loss of generality (since
\ell
is independent of
\epsilon
) we may assume that
\epsilon<\frac{\ell}{(\ell+2+2r^{-1})}

We claim that in this case, we have
\mu(A_{1}\cap A_{2})>0
.
Indeed, we calculate using the estimates above

\displaystyle\mu(A_{1}\cap A_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\mu(A_{1})+\mu(A_{2})-\mu(A)

\displaystyle\geq\mu(A)-2\epsilon\mu(E)+\mu(A)-\frac{2\epsilon}{r}\mu(E)-\mu(A)

\displaystyle>\left(\ell(1-\epsilon)-2\epsilon-\frac{2\epsilon}{r}\right)\mu(E)

\displaystyle=\left(\ell-\left(\ell+2+\frac{2}{r}\right)\epsilon\right)\mu(E)

\displaystyle>0.

Finally, define

A^{\prime}=(E\cap A)\cap g(E\cap B)\subset E.

Then by construction we have
g\cdot A^{\prime}\subset E
,

\mu(A^{\prime})\geq\mu(A_{1}\cap A_{2})>0
,
and moreover
\frac{d(g\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}(\omega)=r
for all
\omega\in A^{\prime}
.
Hence
r\in r(\Omega,G,\mu)
as claimed.

To prove the general case, pick an arbitrary finite path
\zeta
with
s(\zeta)=*
and
r(\zeta)=v
.
Then
\zeta\xi,\zeta\eta\in\Path_{*}^{n+|\zeta|}({\mathcal{G}})
, so
w(\zeta\xi)/w(\zeta\eta)\in r(\Omega,G,\mu)
.
But since
w(\zeta\xi)=w(\zeta)w(\xi)
and similarly for
w(\zeta\eta)
, the claim follows.
∎

We note that the Krieger ratio set can more generally be described in terms of the “asymptotic range” of a Radon–Nikodym cocycle (see
[
FM77a
, Defn. 8.2]
).
But the asymptotic range is equal to the Connes invariant
S

[
Con73
]
of the Krieger factor by
[
FM77b
, Prop. 2.11]
.
Thus we arrive at the following corollary.

Corollary A.17
.

Let
G_{R}
be the subgroup of
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
generated by the set

\left\{\frac{w(\xi)}{w(\eta)}\middle|n\in{\mathbb{N}},\,\,\xi,\eta\in\Path_{*}^{n}({\mathcal{G}}),\,r(\xi)=r(\eta)\right\}.

Then the Connes invariant
S(M)
is given by the closure of
G_{R}
.

Proof.

From the proof of Theorem
A.16
it is enough to consider paths starting in
*
.
By the same theorem,
each ratio
\frac{w(\xi)}{w(\eta)}
is in
S(M)
.
Since
S(M)
is a closed subset of
[0,\infty)
, and
S(M)\cap{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
is a multiplicative subgroup of
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, the closure of
G_{R}
is contained in
S(M)
.

Now each
g\in G
is a finite product of operators of the form
u_{\xi_{k},\eta_{k}}
from (
A.9
).
One can then see (for example, using (
A.12
) and the chain rule for Radon–Nikodym derivatives) that all values of
\frac{d(g\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}
are products of ratios of the weights of a pair of paths as above.
Hence
S(M)
cannot be any larger.
∎

A.4.
Application: type of the boundary algebra in the Levin-Wen model

We associate a graph
{\mathcal{G}}
to a unitary fusion category
{\mathcal{C}}
as follows.
The set of vertices is
{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}=\Irr({\mathcal{C}})
, with distinguished vertex
*=1_{\mathcal{C}}
the tensor unit.
For
c_{1},c_{2}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}
,
the set of edges
{\mathcal{G}}^{(1)}
is a disjoint union of orthonormal bases for
{\mathcal{C}}(c_{2}\to a\otimes c_{1}\otimes b)
over
a,b\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})
, with the isometry inner product determined by

\langle f|g\rangle\id_{c_{2}}=f^{\dagger}\circ g
.
Since
{\mathcal{C}}
is rigid, there is an edge between every pair of vertices.
Thus we can define algebras
B_{n}
again as in §
A.1
.

To illustrate this, first consider the case
n=1
.
Recall that
X=\bigoplus_{c\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})}c
.
We can identify an edge
\xi
from
*
to
c
with a morphism
\xi\in{\mathcal{C}}(c\to a\otimes b)
, which we can identify with
\xi\in{\mathcal{C}}(c\to X\otimes X)
.
If
\eta
is another edge from
*
to
c
, we have
\xi\circ\eta^{\dagger}\in\End(X^{\otimes 2})
, which can be identified with
\left|\xi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\eta\right|
.
By considering all
c\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})
, we see that
B_{1}=\End_{\mathcal{C}}(X^{\otimes 2})
.

If
\xi_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}(c_{2}\to a_{1}\otimes c_{1}\otimes b_{1})
and
\xi_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}(c_{3}\to a_{2}\otimes c_{2}\otimes b_{2})
are two edges, the path
\xi:=\xi_{1}\xi_{2}
is defined by
(\id_{a_{2}}\otimes\xi_{1}\otimes\id_{b_{2}})\circ\xi_{2}
.
As before, we can again see
\xi
as an element of
{\mathcal{C}}(c_{3}\to X\otimes X\otimes c_{1}\otimes X\otimes X)
.
Generalising this to paths of length
n
, it follows that each algebra
B_{n}\cong\End_{\mathcal{C}}(X^{\otimes 2n})
.
Moreover, the inclusion
\iota_{n}:B_{n}\hookrightarrow B_{n+1}
as defined in (
A.1
) is given by
\xi\mapsto\id_{X}\otimes\xi\otimes\id_{X}
.

Next we define a weight
w
on
{\mathcal{G}}
.
For vertices, set
w(c):=d_{c}
, where
d_{c}
is the quantum dimension of
c\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})
.
Since an edge from
c_{1}
to
c_{2}
is an element
\eta\in{\mathcal{C}}(c_{2}\to a\otimes c_{1}\otimes b)
for some irreducible
a
and
b
, we can set
w(\eta):=d_{a}d_{b}
.
We see (
A.3
) is satisfied with
\delta=\left(\sum_{c\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})}d_{c}^{2}\right)^{2}
:
for fixed
c_{1}\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b,c_{2}\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})}\sum_{\eta\in\operatorname{ONB}(c_{2}\to a\otimes c_{1}\otimes b)}w(\eta)w(r(\eta))

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c_{2},e\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})}N_{ae}^{c_{2}}N_{c_{1}b}^{e}d_{a}d_{b}d_{c_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,e\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})}N_{c_{1}b}^{e}d_{a}^{2}d_{b}d_{e}=\left(\sum_{a,b\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})}d_{a}^{2}d_{b}^{2}\right)d_{c_{1}}.

Now suppose that
c\prec a\otimes b
for
a,b,c\in\Irr({\mathcal{C}})
.
That is,
c
appears in the direct sum composition of
a\otimes b
.
We call such a triple
(a,b,c)

admissable
.

Lemma A.18
.

The following set

\left\{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}\middle|\,(a,b,c)\text{ is admissable}\right\}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

generates a dense subgroup of
S(M)
.

Proof.

Let
(a,b,c)
be an admissable triple.
Then there exist non-zero

\xi\in{\mathcal{C}}(c\to a\otimes 1\otimes b)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\eta\in{\mathcal{C}}(c\to c\otimes 1\otimes 1).

We can choose both of them to be edges from
1
to
c
, and identify
\xi
and
\eta
with paths of length one.
Note that
w(\xi)=d_{a}d_{b}
and
w(\eta)=d_{c}
.
Hence by Corollary
A.17
it follows that
\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}\in S(M)
.

Now let
\xi=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}
be a path from
*
to
c
, with
\xi_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}(c_{i+1}\to a_{i}\otimes c_{i}\otimes b_{i})
, and thus
c_{1}=*
and
c_{n+1}=c
.
Using the fusion rules and semisimplicity, it follows that such a (non-zero)
\xi_{i}
only exists if there is some
k
such that
(c_{i},b_{i},k)
and
(a_{i},k,c_{i+1})
are both admissable.
Using the first part, this means that

\left(\frac{d_{c_{i}}d_{b_{i}}}{d_{k}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{d_{a_{i}}d_{k}}{d_{c_{i+1}}}\right)=\frac{d_{a_{i}}d_{b_{i}}d_{c_{i}}}{d_{c_{i+1}}}

is in the generated group.
Taking the product over all
i=1,\dots,n
gives
w(\xi)/d_{c}
.
If
\eta
is another path from
*
to
c
of the same length, the same argument gives that
w(\eta)/d_{c}
is in the generated group.
Thus
w(\xi)/w(\eta)
is as well.
The claim then follows from Corollary
A.17
.
∎

From the characterization of the net of algebras
(B_{n})
, it follows that the limit algebra
B
is the same as that of the fusion categorical net
I\mapsto{\mathfrak{F}}(I)
for the Levin–Wen model studied in §
5.2
.
Using the proof of Lemma
A.18
and the observations made earlier, we see that the state
\phi
defined on
B
via (
A.6
) is exactly the canonical boundary state
\psi_{{\mathfrak{F}}}
, which is described explicitly in Proposition
5.5
.
(Alternatively, since the dynamics (
A.5
) on
B
is inverse to the dynamics (
5.7
) on
{\mathfrak{F}}
, the
\beta=-1
KMS state
\phi
on
B
agrees with the
\beta=1
KMS state
\psi_{\mathfrak{F}}
on
{\mathfrak{F}}
.)
Thus we have obtained the following lemma.

Lemma A.19
.

The von Neumann algebra
M
is isomorphic to the boundary algebra
{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime\prime}
for the Levin–Wen model for
{\mathcal{C}}
.

We are now in a position to determine the type of the boundary algebra.
Since
S(M)
is closed in
[0,\infty)
and
S(M)\cap{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
is a subgroup of
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
it follows that
S(M)
must be one of the following four cases
[
Con73
]
:

•

S(M)=\{1\}
, in which case
M
is semi-finite.
From Lemma
A.18
this is true if and only if all
d_{a}=1
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{C}}
is pointed.
In this case, it is also easy to see directly that the state
\phi
defined in §
A.2
is a trace.

•

S(M)=\{0,1\}
, in which case
M
is type
\rm{III}_{0}
.
Note that
g\cdot\mu
and
\mu
are mutually absolutely continuous.
Hence the Radon–Nikodym derivative
\frac{d(g\cdot\mu)}{d\mu}
cannot be zero on a non-neglible set.
By Corollary
A.17
, if
0\in S(M)
, it must be the limit of non-zero points in
S(M)
.
This is in contradiction with
1
being the only other point in
S(M)
, so the type
\rm{III}_{0}
case cannot occur.

•

S(M)=\{0\}\cup\left\{\lambda^{n}\middle|n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\right\}
for some
\lambda\in(0,1)
, in which case
M
is of type
\rm{III}_{\lambda}
.
Note that Lemma
A.18
implies that for each admissable triple
(a,b,c)
, there is an integer
Z_{ab}^{c}
such that

\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}=\lambda^{Z_{ab}^{c}},

and the set
\{Z_{ab}^{c}\}
generates
{\mathbb{Z}}
as a group.

•

S(M)=[0,\infty)
, in which case
M
is of type
\rm{III}_{1}
.
This is equivalent to the set

(A.20)

\left\{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}\middle|\,(a,b,c)\text{ admissable}\right\}

generating a dense subgroup of
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.

This result refines Theorem
5.9
.
